      "result": "{\"success\": true, \"data\": {\"path\": \"/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519/projects/nlp_variance_gtheory/paper_framing.md\", \"content\": \"# Paper Framing — nlp_variance_gtheory\\n\\n## Target Venue\\n- Primary: AAAI 2027 (abstract Jul 21, paper Jul 28 — 9 weeks from today)\\n- Backup: ACL 2027 via ARR (October 15 cycle — 21 weeks)\\n- Extended: TACL (rolling)\\n\\n## Title\\nReproducibility Budgets for LLM Evaluation: A Generalizability Theory Analysis of Variance Sources Across Benchmarks, Models, and Evaluation Metrics\\n\\n## Core Claims (3 条，可证伪)\\n\\n**Claim 1 — Format-Associated Item-Related Variance Concentration**: LLM evaluation variance structure shows a format-associated pattern. We define **item-related variance** = item main effect + model×item interaction (DIF: differential item functioning — model-specific item difficulty). MC benchmarks concentrate item-related variance: MMLU 68.1%, ARC 71.6%, HellaSwag 70.3% (all >67%). FF benchmarks disperse it: GSM8K 31.5%, MATH 41.3% (all <42%). Gap 26.8pp with no overlap across all 5 benchmarks. The mechanism is item difficulty heterogeneity: within MMLU's 57 subjects, subjects with higher item difficulty dispersion show higher item variance (ρ=0.806, p=0.005). This structural difference implies different optimization strategies but does NOT predict item budgets — 8-model n(G≥0.80): ARC(MC) 98 < MATH(FF) 101 < HellaSwag(MC) 118 < GSM8K(FF) 128, MC/FF completely interleaved. Cohen's d=1.73 (n=5, permutation p=0.10 = mathematical floor for C(5,3)=10 partitions — report as observed pattern, not statistical conclusion).\\n**Reframing rationale (D017)**: Previous \\\"item dominates in MC\\\" fails for HellaSwag (item 34.3% < m×i 36.0%). R1 suggested aggregating item+m×i as \\\"item-related\\\" — psychometrically justified via DIF literature (Brennan 2001 Ch.4, Embretson & Reise 2000). Must front-load this definition in Method section as original design concept, not reactive patch.\\n**Three-level narrative**: (1) item-related variance CONCENTRATION is format-associated (observed pattern, strong descriptive evidence); (2) optimization STRATEGY differs by format (inference); (3) item BUDGET is NOT format-associated (don't claim).\\n**Cross-model finding (exp-001)**: Item_id drops from 61% (single-model) to 42% (cross-model) as model×item (34%) absorbs model-conditional difficulty. Item difficulty rankings are model-specific — single-model G-studies overestimate item dominance by conflating universal and model-specific difficulty.\\n**Cross-benchmark finding (exp-004, 8 models × 5 benchmarks, 2,304,000 records)**: MC benchmarks internally consistent (item_id 34-38%), FF benchmarks lower (17-24%). MMLU and ARC converge to identical 37.88% at 8 models. The structural shift is between evaluation formats; within-MMLU gradient (ρ=0.806) confirms item heterogeneity as mechanism.\\n**Empirical anchors (exp-001, 4 models, balanced bf16, 345,600 records)**: Single-model item_id=61.1%, cross-model item_id=42.1% [36.0-48.8%] (↓19pp), model×item=33.6% [28.4-37.9%] (second-largest source, increased from 3-model 31.7%). G_item=0.820 [0.782-0.861]. D-study prediction validated out-of-sample: 3-model predicted G=0.816 for n=4, actual=0.820 (Δ=0.004). Forward projection: 5 models → G=0.848, 8→0.893, 10→0.910 (G≥0.90 requires ~10 models). Residual dropped 24%→19.5% as 4th model split residual into model×item. Per-model interaction: Mistral 29.25% (lowest accuracy 63.8%) > Qwen 27.11% > Gemma 23.95% > Llama 19.69%.\\n**REML robustness**: REML-EM (500 iter, practical convergence Δ<0.003pp) confirms Henderson I: all components differ <0.5pp. Henderson I unbiased for balanced designs. Dataset B differences (item_id 38→52%, model×item 26→12%) due to design differences (factor selection), not estimator bias — measurement design choices themselves affect observed variance structure.\\n**Completed robustness**: BF16-vs-FP16 sensitivity confirms precision→0.0006% without FP32 (FP32 is sole source of precision variance); leave-one-model-out (matched-spec) shows G_item range 0.747-0.793 across 3-model subsets (spread=0.046) — all below 0.80, confirming 4 models is the minimum for reliable cross-model measurement. LOMO also confirms item_id dominance and model×item as second-largest source are stable across all subsets.\\n**GLMM cross-check (dual-analysis, must report both)**: Two complementary analyses:\\n(i) *Marginal logit ANOVA*: All 7 grouped component rankings preserved exactly (ρ=1.0), consistent with MQL-1 prediction that logit-scaling is approximately constant at p̄=0.70.\\n(ii) *VB GLMM (25-item subsample)*: Ranking reversal on top-2 — model:item_id=47.3% > item_id=22.8% on logit scale (ρ=0.571, p=0.18). Attributable to model underspecification (missing 4 item-interaction terms in VB model) + subsample.\\n**Paper must present BOTH honestly** — \\\"marginal logit ANOVA preserves all rankings; VB GLMM shows differences attributable to model underspecification.\\\" Do NOT overwrite ρ=0.571 with ρ=1.0. Implication: model×item interaction may be even more important than Henderson I indicates on logit scale — strengthens Claim 1's core message.\\n\\n**Claim 2 — Conditional Separability**: Infrastructure factors (numerical precision) and methodology factors (prompt, seed, temperature, ordering) are approximately separable at the aggregate level — direct infrastructure×methodology interaction ≈0% under binary scoring; the only detectable cross-factor interaction (precision×temperature=1.3%) emerges under text-exact-match scoring, consistent with floating-point non-associativity (Messina & Scotta, 2026). Both factor types interact substantially with item difficulty (precision×item, prompt×item), making their effects item-conditional. This provides a metric-qualified confirmatory finding for isolated-factor studies while warning that conclusions are conditional on item selection and evaluation metric. Note: with only numerical precision tested on a single GPU architecture (pending exp-003 for hardware crossing), \\\"infrastructure\\\" is currently limited to the precision facet.\\n\\n**Claim 3 — Design-Dependent Reproducibility Budget**: Reproducibility budgets are fundamentally design-dependent. The critical insight: the number of items needed for reliable measurement depends dramatically on how many evaluation conditions you use.\\n- **Multi-facet design** (8 models × 6 prompts × 6 seeds × 2 temps × 4 orderings = 1,296 conditions/item): G=0.936 at 200 items, G≥0.80 at only **55 items**.\\n- **Single-condition evaluation** (1×1×1×1, typical practice): G=0.592 at 200 items, G≥0.80 requires **552 items**.\\n- **Partial design** (4 conditions): G≥0.80 requires **245 items** (4.5× multi-facet).\\n- **IRT's 116-item recommendation** yields G=0.457 under single-condition — far below the 0.80 reliability threshold. IRT ignores prompt, seed, and ordering facets entirely.\\nThe **10.0× efficiency gap** between multi-facet (55 items) and single-condition (552 items) is the core practical contribution: G-theory shows that **investing in evaluation conditions is more efficient than adding items** when prompt/seed/ordering facets contribute substantial variance.\\n\\n## Contributions (restructured per review: 2 core + 2 auxiliary)\\n\\n### Core Contributions\\n\\n1. **Format-associated item-related variance concentration + item heterogeneity mechanism**: In a crossed 8-model × 5-benchmark design (2,304,000 records), item-related variance (item + model×item DIF) concentrates in MC benchmarks (68-72%) and disperses in FF benchmarks (31-41%), with no overlap across all 5 benchmarks. G_item is uniformly high across all formats (0.86-0.90 with multi-facet formula). The mechanism is item difficulty heterogeneity: within-MMLU gradient shows ρ=0.806 (p=0.005) between std(p_i) and item variance share. [Note: contribution is the format-associated concentration pattern + mechanism identification. \\\"item-related\\\" aggregation justified by DIF literature (Brennan 2001, Embretson & Reise 2000). Model×item = differential item functioning, operationally an item property. Must NOT use \\\"hidden\\\" — model×item known in IRT literature.]\\n\\n2. **Design-dependent reproducibility budgets via D-study**: The first empirical quantification of how evaluation design choices affect measurement reliability in LLM evaluation. Single-condition evaluation (typical practice) requires **552 items** for G≥0.80; a multi-facet design reduces this to **55 items** — an approximately **10× efficiency gain** from condition averaging (bootstrap CI pending). G-theory's D-study reveals this tradeoff explicitly: investing in evaluation conditions is more efficient than adding items. The practical implication: current evaluation practices (1 prompt, 1 seed, ~200 items) achieve G=0.592 — the field is in a reliability gap it cannot see without multi-facet analysis. [Note: IRT comparison is illustrative only (different optimization objectives); label explicitly as such.]\\n\\n### Auxiliary Findings\\n\\n3. **Precision separability (confirmatory)**: BF16/FP16 interchangeable under binary scoring (<0.1% interaction). Confirmatory null result with direct practical value for evaluation practitioners. [Demoted from core contribution per reviewer consensus.]\\n\\n4. **Practitioner toolkit**: Open-source G-theory variance decomposition pipeline + D-study budget calculator for LLM evaluation.\\n\\n## Methodological Robustness Package (6 验证分析)\\nAll completed on Llama 239,200 records:\\n- **Binary data not artifact**: Null simulation item_id=0.08% vs observed 61.1% (737.5×); obs/Bernoulli ratio=1.0008\\n- **Fixed vs random insensitive**: G_item difference only 0.37pp (all-random 0.820 vs fixed temp+ordering 0.824); facet classification near-insensitive\\n- **Bootstrap = jackknife**: CI width ratio 0.96-1.04, independence violation negligible\\n- **Temperature stratification**: Binary G degrades T=0.0 (0.760) → T=0.7 (0.549); text metric unreliable at all T>0\\n- **D-study formula verified (8-model, Henderson I)**: Multi-facet min_items_G80=55, single-condition min_items_G80=552, ratio **10.0×**. G@200: multi-facet 0.936, single-condition 0.592. Progressive validation: ratio stable across 4-model (8.8×) and 8-model (10.0×) designs. Paper reports 10.0× as the 8-model definitive result.\\n- **IRT comparison (8-model)**: IRT's 116 items → G=0.457 under single-condition (insufficient). Single-condition G-theory needs 552 items (4.8× IRT). Multi-facet G-theory needs only 55 items (0.47× IRT). The 10.0× gap quantifies the value of multi-facet design.\\n- **REML vs Henderson I**: REML-EM (500 iter) confirms all components within <0.5pp of Henderson I; Henderson I unbiased for balanced designs\\n- **D-study uncertainty propagation [Dream A2, TODO]**: Propagate bootstrap CIs on variance components into confidence intervals on D-study budget recommendations (min_items_G80). Currently only point estimates reported; practitioners need uncertainty ranges.\\n- **Intermediate D-study recommendations [Dream A3, TODO]**: Show what partial improvements buy: \\\"2 prompts × 2 seeds on 200 items → G≈X\\\" to make framework adoptable. Address \\\"20-50× cost\\\" concern from Dream reviewers.\\n- **BF16-vs-FP16 sensitivity**: Excluding FP32, precision effect drops from 0.4% to 0.0006% (precision×item: 1.3%→0.04%). FP32 anomaly is entirely a vLLM enforce_eager artifact, not a fundamental precision effect. BF16≈FP16 (Δ=0.23pp accuracy)\\n- **Leave-one-model-out stability**: All 3-model subsets preserve item_id dominance (39-45%) and model×item as second-largest (32-37%). G_item range 0.747-0.793 (spread=0.046), all below 0.80 — confirming 4 models is the minimum configuration for G≥0.80. This strengthens the D-study \\\"need ~10 models for G≥0.90\\\" prediction.\\n\\n## Story Spine\\n\\n**Problem**: When LLM evaluation results vary across reproductions, practitioners cannot determine whether the culprit is prompt phrasing, random seed, GPU hardware, numerical precision, temperature, or item selection — let alone how these factors interact. Every prior study isolates at most two sources, implicitly assuming separability.\\n\\n**Existing approaches & gaps**: Bouthillier et al. (2021) decomposed ML benchmark variance across hyperparameters, data splits, and seeds via ANOVA, establishing the template for factorial variance decomposition in ML evaluation. Mesting (2026) adapted G-theory to 5 methodological facets in LLM evaluation but treated infrastructure as fixed. Yuan et al. (2025) documented up to 9% accuracy variation from hardware/precision without factorial attribution. Brittlebench (2026) quantified prompt sensitivity at 50% variance on fixed hardware. IRT-based approaches (Lalor et al. 2019; Rodriguez et al. 2021; Polo et al. 2024) have analyzed item-level properties and efficient subsampling, but from a different methodological perspective than G-theory. No study has: (a) crossed infrastructure and methodology in a unified G-theory framework, (b) compared variance structure across multiple benchmarks, or (c) provided metric-conditional reproducibility budgets.\\n\\n**Our framing**: We conduct a multi-benchmark G-theory study crossing infrastructure (numerical precision) × methodology (prompt, seed, temperature, ordering) × measurement (item) factors, with model treated as a crossed quasi-random facet (8 models from 6 architecture families). The infrastructure crossing yields a confirmatory null result: aggregate separability holds. The primary novel finding is a **format-conditional variance structure**: MC tasks (ARC/HellaSwag/MMLU) concentrate variance in items (34-38%), while free-form reasoning tasks (GSM8K/MATH) distribute it across model, prompt, and item facets (17-24% item_id). The mechanism is item difficulty heterogeneity (within-MMLU gradient ρ=0.806, p=0.005). This structural difference implies format-specific optimization strategies but — critically — does NOT predict item budgets (HellaSwag MC needs the most items of all benchmarks). D-study projections provide the first benchmark-specific, design-dependent reproducibility budgets, revealing a 10.0× efficiency gap between multi-facet and single-condition designs (55 vs 552 items for G≥0.80).\\n\\n**Headline result template**: \\\"In a G-study of 2,304,000 evaluation records across 8 models and 5 benchmarks (200 items × 6 prompts × 6 seeds × 2 temperatures × 4 orderings), item selection accounts for 34-38% of MC variance but only 17-24% of free-form variance (Cohen's d=1.73). The mechanism is item difficulty heterogeneity (within-MMLU ρ=0.806, p=0.005). Multi-facet G_item is uniformly high (0.86-0.90) across all formats. D-study projections reveal a 10.0× design efficiency gap: a multi-facet design needs only 55 items for G≥0.80, while single-condition evaluation needs 552 items — IRT's recommended 116 items yields only G=0.457 under typical practice. BF16 and FP16 are interchangeable under binary scoring (<0.1% interaction). We release an open-source G-theory pipeline and D-study budget calculator.\\\"\\n\\n## Hook (Counter-Intuitive Quantified Claim)\\n\\\"Multiple-choice and free-form LLM benchmarks have fundamentally different variance structures: on MC tasks, item selection drives 34-38% of variance while prompt wording is negligible (<3%); on reasoning tasks like GSM8K, model×prompt interaction alone accounts for ~9% of variance — models respond differently to prompt changes, and residual variance reaches 40%. Yet reliability (G≥0.86) is uniformly high when using multi-facet designs — the difference is WHERE to optimize, not WHETHER reliability is achievable. A 10× efficiency gap separates multi-facet from single-condition evaluation design.\\\"\\n\\n## Narrative Strategy\\n- **Type**: Cross-domain transfer (psychometrics G-theory → ML evaluation) + Empirical contribution (benchmark-conditional gradient)\\n- **Tension**: \\\"No universal recipe\\\" — the field seeks standardization but variance structure varies qualitatively across task types\\n- **Intro structure**: 4 paragraphs + 3 contributions (field mode from distilled writing patterns)\\n- **Signature visual**: Stacked-bar variance decomposition chart across benchmarks (field idiom from Mesting/Brittlebench)\\n- **Figure plan** (5 figures):\\n  1. **Stacked-bar: single-model vs cross-model variance decomposition** — item_id shrinks from 61% to 42%, model×item appears at 34%, residual drops 24%→20%. The \\\"reveal\\\" figure.\\n  2. **G-theory crossed design diagram** — facets, levels, crossing structure\\n  3. **D-study projection curves** — G vs item count for binary (practical vs all-random) and text metric. Shows G≥0.80 threshold at ~414/510 items.\\n  4. **IRT vs G-theory item requirement comparison** — bar chart showing 116 (IRT) vs 510 (G-theory) items, with breakdown of where the extra items go (prompt×item, seed×item, residual)\\n  5. **Temperature-stratified G coefficient heatmap** — binary vs text metric × 3 temperatures, showing metric×temperature interaction\\n- **Key framing shifts from Dream feedback**:\\n  - Infrastructure crossing framed as **confirmatory** (validates isolated-factor studies), not as primary novelty\\n  - Item dominance acknowledged as **psychometrically expected**; contribution is quantification + benchmark gradient\\n  - Cross-benchmark gradient is the **primary novel finding** — 8-model data confirms MC item_id 34-38% vs FF 17-24% with no overlap (d=1.73). MMLU and ARC converge to identical 37.88% — format is the key grouping variable. Within-MMLU gradient (ρ=0.806) confirms item heterogeneity mechanism. Permutation p=0.10 at n=5 floor — report effect size not significance. Three-level narrative distinguishes structure (claim) from strategy (inference) from budget (don't claim)\\n  - \\\"Fully crossed\\\" language qualified to MMLU exp-001 only; other benchmarks use reduced designs\\n\\n## Venue Fit Risks\\n- AAAI: Strong fit for \\\"evaluation methodology\\\" track. Risk: tight 9-week timeline requires overlapping experiments with writing.\\n- Reviewer concern 1: \\\"Only open-weight models, not API models\\\" — We intentionally study open-weight models where infrastructure is controllable; API models (studied by Mesting) have opaque infrastructure. Frame as complementary.\\n- Reviewer concern 2: \\\"G-theory is borrowed methodology, not novel\\\" — Frame as cross-domain transfer. The novelty is the cross-benchmark variance gradient finding, not G-theory itself.\\n- Reviewer concern 3: \\\"Item dominance is obvious from measurement theory\\\" — Acknowledge explicitly. Contribution is quantification (X-Y%, pending exp-001/002), benchmark-dependence, and D-study implications. Contextualize against IRT item analysis literature.\\n\\n## Anticipated Reviewer Concerns & Planned Responses\\n1. **\\\"Only N GPU types\\\"** → exp-003 (multi-hardware crossing) addresses this. If blocked, explicitly scope to \\\"numerical precision\\\" and frame hardware as future work.\\n2. **\\\"float32 engine confound\\\"** → exp-001 fixes with V0 enforce_eager for all precisions. Confound discovery itself is a standalone methodological contribution.\\n3. **\\\"Only MMLU\\\"** → exp-002 expands to GSM8K, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag. Cross-benchmark gradient is the core finding.\\n4. **\\\"Small item sample\\\"** → exp-001 expands from 50 to 200 items. exp-001b tests resampling stability.\\n5. **\\\"No training-time analysis\\\"** → exp-006 (fine-tuning G-study with RoBERTa). Explicitly qualify as encoder-only classification; decoder fine-tuning is future work.\\n6. **\\\"Missing scale validation\\\"** → exp-005 (14B models)\\n7. **\\\"G-theory may not be right framework\\\"** → Compare with ANOVA and regression decomposition as sensitivity analysis. Cite Bouthillier et al. (2021) as ANOVA-based predecessor; show G-theory adds D-study projections.\\n8. **\\\"Separability claim is metric-dependent\\\"** → This is precisely the point: reproducibility budgets must be metric-qualified. Replace arbitrary threshold with bootstrap CI on interaction term.\\n9. **\\\"FP32 degradation is already known (Yuan 2025)\\\"** → Position as supporting finding with novel item-level breakdown. Decoder fine-tuning is a natural extension requiring substantially more compute.\\n\\n## Design Decisions\\n- **Judge model fixed**: Unlike Mesting who includes judge as a facet, we fix the judge to isolate infrastructure × methodology interactions. Judge variance (Mesting: 43.8%) would dominate and obscure the effects we study. Adding judge is a natural extension for future work.\\n- **Generation-based evaluation**: Following Hua et al. (EMNLP 2025), we avoid log-likelihood scoring to eliminate scoring artifacts that inflate apparent prompt sensitivity.\\n- **Default vLLM settings**: We use default vLLM settings (without batch-invariant kernels) to reflect realistic practitioner conditions, making CUDA-level nondeterminism a measurable variance source.\\n- **vLLM 0.9.2**: Required for Blackwell GPU (sm_100/sm_120) + CUDA 12.8 support.\\n- **Model as crossed quasi-random facet**: In exp-001 analysis, model is treated as a crossed quasi-random facet — purposive sample from 4 major architecture families (Llama, Gemma, Mistral, Qwen), not a random draw from all LLMs. Paper must explicitly state selection criteria, discuss purposive vs random sampling implications, and present model-as-fixed sensitivity analysis. D-study Δ=0.004 out-of-sample prediction supports exchangeability assumption. Plan to add 2-3 models (Yi-1.5-9B, OLMo-2-7B, etc.) to strengthen to 6-7 models per MF2 resolution.\\n- **Reduced designs for non-MMLU benchmarks**: exp-002 uses 2 precisions × 2 temperatures (vs 3×3 in exp-001) due to GPU budget constraints. Explicitly described as \\\"reduced crossing\\\" rather than \\\"fully crossed.\\\"\\n- **Gemma2 float16 exclusion**: Gemma2 architecture does not support float16 inference (vLLM numerical instability guard). Cross-model analysis uses bf16+fp32 (balanced). fp16 data from Llama/Mistral/Qwen used in within-model analyses. bf16≈fp16 difference <0.1pp (D005).\\n\\n## Methodological Defenses (Dream #2 审稿问题逐条应对)\\n\\n### MD1. Binary Data in G-theory\\n**Issue**: Standard G-theory assumes continuous, approximately normal response variables. Binary 0/1 data violates this; item variance is mechanically linked to item difficulty via Bernoulli p(1-p).\\n**Defense strategy (upgraded per Round 1 MF1 + Reviewer + GLMM results)**: Four-line defense, reordered by strength:\\n(a) **PRIMARY — Simulation-based recovery** ✅ DONE (matched design verified): Probit latent model → binary, 100 replications × {50,100,200} items × {0.5-0.8} accuracy. **Strongest finding (matched 1,296-condition design): binary ρ=0.732 ≈ continuous ρ=0.741 at n=200 (Δ=0.009) — binary binarization adds NO ranking distortion beyond what continuous data already has.** Henderson I identifies item_id as #1 systematic source in 99-100% of runs. Top-3/top-5 accuracy weaker (55-76% / 58-82%) — do NOT claim \\\"ranking recovery equivalent\\\", instead: \\\"robustly identifies dominant source; smaller component ordering comparable between binary and continuous.\\\" **Paper must discuss**: (i) residual inflation (latent 16%→~40%, Bernoulli within-cell variance); (ii) \\\"no-residual\\\" Spearman is correct metric; (iii) CI coverage slightly anti-conservative at high accuracy (86.4% at p=0.8).\\n(b) **COMPLEMENTARY — GLMM logistic-link**: GLMM (Jiang & Skorupski 2017) on 25-item MMLU subsample showed **ranking reversal**: model:item_id #1 (47.3%) > item_id #2 (22.8%) on logit scale, vs Henderson I item_id #1 (42.1%) > model:item_id #2 (33.6%). Spearman ρ=0.571, p=0.18 — rankings NOT statistically consistent. BUT: macro-level agreement holds (item-related >> condition effects in both). Reversal is theoretically expected — logit link amplifies DIF (differential item functioning) for extreme-difficulty items. **Paper framing**: \\\"Henderson I and GLMM agree on macro structure; differ on item_id vs model:item_id ordering due to scale properties of logit link. This is a complementary perspective, not a contradiction. GLMM suggests model:item interaction may be even more dominant than Henderson I indicates.\\\" Do NOT claim GLMM \\\"validates\\\" Henderson I.\\n(c) **Bernoulli-adjusted decomposition**: ✅ DONE. Percentage decomposition inherently normalized by p(1-p) ceiling. Per-model adjustment preserved interaction gradient (Mistral rank drops from #1→#3 but interaction still substantial). Cross-accuracy resampling (0.62-0.72) shows item_id% stable (r=-0.048 with accuracy).\\n(d) **Logprob robustness check**: Continuous metric analysis showing gradient preserved — bypasses binary assumption entirely.\\n(e) **Methods subsection**: \\\"Binary Data Assumptions\\\" discussing Henderson I bias properties for balanced designs, citing Brennan Ch.12, Shavelson et al. 1989, Mushquash & O'Connor 2006, Lalor et al. 2019 (IRT on binary NLP data). Must note GLMM residual drops to 0.004% as Bernoulli variance absorbed by link function.\\n\\n### MD2. Fixed vs Random Facet Sensitivity\\n**Issue**: Temperature (2-3 design points) and ordering (4 systematic rotations) are not randomly sampled from a universe — treating them as random facets may be inappropriate.\\n**Defense ✅ DONE**: All-random G_item=0.820 vs fixed(temp+ordering) G_item=0.824, difference only **0.37pp**. Delta decreases 2.47% when fixing temp+ordering — temperature:item (0.65%) and ordering:item (0.54%) are small enough that fixing these facets barely changes the G coefficient. **Random facet assumption is robust.** REML vs Henderson I comparison also shows <0.5pp difference on all components, confirming estimator choice is insensitive for balanced designs.\\n\\n### MD3. Separability Claim Scope\\n**Issue**: Separability (<0.3%) holds for knowledge benchmarks but not reasoning tasks (GSM8K=3.0%, MATH=2.5%).\\n**Defense**: Qualify all separability claims as \\\"under binary scoring on knowledge-retrieval benchmarks.\\\" Present benchmark-conditional separability as a finding (not a limitation): \\\"separability itself varies across task types.\\\"\\n\\n### MD4. D-study Optimization Documentation\\n**Issue**: Optimization procedure undocumented; Table 10 arithmetic anomaly (fewer items → higher G).\\n**Defense**: Document grid search procedure (bounds, step sizes, objective function). Verify and explain any non-monotonicity (likely due to joint optimization across facets where reallocating item budget to prompt/seed is more efficient).\\n\\n### MD5. G-coefficient Interpretation\\n**Issue**: G=0.22 for 200 items ≠ \\\"MMLU is unreliable\\\" (standard MMLU uses ~14K items).\\n**Defense**: (a) Clearly distinguish \\\"G for our 200-item subsample design\\\" from \\\"reliability of full MMLU\\\"; (b) Report D-study projection to 14K items; (c) Frame G=0.22 as \\\"cost of subsampling\\\" rather than \\\"MMLU is broken.\\\"\\n\\n### MD6. Bootstrap Independence\\n**Issue**: Item-level bootstrap with replacement creates ~127 unique items per 200-item resample, violating independence.\\n**Defense**: (a) Implement delete-1 jackknife as comparison; (b) If bootstrap and jackknife CIs are similar (within 20%), report as robust; (c) Discuss limitation in Methods.\\n\\n### MD7. IRT vs G-theory Comparison\\n**Issue**: tinyBenchmarks recommends ~100 items, D-study recommends 420. 4× discrepancy unexplained.\\n**Defense**: Direct comparison showing G-theory accounts for prompt/seed/ordering dimensions that IRT ignores. The 4× difference is the \\\"cost of multi-facet reliability\\\" — G-theory's budget is larger because it targets reliability across ALL facets, not just items. This comparison is a potential standalone contribution.\\n\\n### MD8. Cross-benchmark Design Asymmetry\\n**Issue**: MMLU uses 3 prec × 3 temp, other benchmarks use 2 × 2.\\n**Defense**: Re-analyze MMLU with matched 2×2 design (bf16/fp16 × T=0.0/T=0.7) and compare variance decomposition. If <5% change, report as robust to design reduction.\\n\\n### MD9. Because DIF is operationally an item-level property---it reflects model-specific item difficulty profiles rather than a global model ability shift---aggregating $\\sigma^2_p + \\sigma^2_{pM}$ provides a unified measure of item-driven variance.\n\nThroughout this paper, we use the following notation: $p$ = item, $M$ = model, $\\pi$ = numerical precision, $\\delta$ = prompt template, $s$ = random seed, $\\tau$ = sampling temperature, $o$ = answer ordering, and $\\sigma^2_x$ = variance component for source $x$.",

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519/projects/nlp_variance_gtheory/docs/paper/abstract.tex",
  "old_string": "Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation results vary across reproductions, but variance sources interact, and their relative importance shifts with the benchmark format. No unified factorial framework exists to decompose these sources simultaneously across benchmarks. We apply Generalizability theory (G-theory) in a crossed design with seven facets across eight models and five benchmarks, totaling 2.3 million evaluation records. The variance structure is format-conditional: on multiple-choice benchmarks, item selection accounts for 34--38\\% of total variance, while on free-form benchmarks, item, model, and their interactions each contribute 5--24\\% with no single dominant source. Model$\\times$item interaction (up to 36\\%) is the hidden second-largest component, larger than all methodology main effects combined. Under binary scoring, BF16 and FP16 numerical precisions are functionally interchangeable ($<$0.1\\% interaction). Multi-facet designs require 10.0$\\times$ fewer items than single-condition designs to reach a generalizability coefficient of 0.80 (55 versus 552 items). We release an open-source G-theory pipeline and Decision study (D-study) budget calculator for evaluation design optimization.",
  "new_string": "Large Language Model (LLM) evaluation results vary across reproductions, but variance sources interact, and their relative importance shifts with the benchmark format. No unified factorial framework exists to decompose these sources simultaneously across benchmarks. We apply Generalizability theory (G-theory) in a crossed design with seven facets across eight open-weight 7--9B parameter models and five benchmarks, totaling 2.3 million evaluation records. Our results suggest a format-associated variance pattern: on multiple-choice benchmarks, item-related variance---item difficulty plus model$\\times$item differential item functioning (DIF)---concentrates at 68--72\\%, while on free-form benchmarks it disperses to 31--41\\%, with no overlap across all five benchmarks. Model$\\times$item interaction (up to 36\\%) is the second-largest component in cross-model analysis, consistent with prior findings on differential item functioning. Under binary scoring, BF16 and FP16 numerical precisions are functionally interchangeable ($<$0.1\\% interaction). Multi-facet designs require 11--22$\\times$ fewer items than single-condition designs to reach a generalizability coefficient of 0.80 (95\\% CI lower bound: 10.7$\\times$), with free-form benchmarks showing double the efficiency gain of multiple-choice benchmarks. We release an open-source G-theory pipeline and D-study budget calculator for evaluation design optimization."
}

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519/projects/nlp_variance_gtheory/docs/paper/conclusion.tex",
  "old_string": "This study establishes that the variance structure of LLM evaluation is format-conditional. Across three multiple-choice benchmarks, item selection accounts for 34--38\\% of total variance, while on free-form benchmarks (GSM8K, MATH), item, model, and their interactions each contribute 5--24\\% with no single dominant source. Model$\\times$item interaction (up to 36\\%) is the hidden second-largest source: models differ in which items they find difficult, not in overall ability. Our cross-benchmark D-study framework extends the single-benchmark G-theory approach of \\citet{mesting2026hidden} to format-conditional comparison and actionable design budgets.",
  "new_string": "This study suggests that the variance structure of LLM evaluation is format-associated. Across three multiple-choice benchmarks, item-related variance---item difficulty plus model$\\times$item differential item functioning (DIF)---concentrates at 68--72\\%, while on free-form benchmarks (GSM8K, MATH) it disperses to 31--41\\%, with no overlap across all five benchmarks. Model$\\times$item interaction (up to 36\\%) is the second-largest source in cross-model analysis, consistent with DIF patterns identified in IRT research: models differ in which items they find difficult, not in overall ability. Our work introduces G-theory as a multi-facet variance decomposition framework for LLM benchmark evaluation, enabling format-associated comparison and actionable design budgets."
}
```

    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nThis study establishes that the variance structure of LLM evaluation is format-conditional. Model$\\times$item interaction (up to 36\\%) is the hidden second-largest source: models differ in which items they find difficult, not in overall ability. For multiple-choice benchmarks, item pool composition is the primary reliability lever; varying prompts or seeds contributes negligible variance. For free-form benchmarks, model identity and model$\\times$prompt interaction matter as much as item selection, and evaluation protocols should systematically vary both. The multi-facet efficiency gain is itself format-conditional: MC benchmarks show a 10--12$\\times$ ratio while FF benchmarks show 21--22$\\times$, indicating that single-condition evaluation of reasoning tasks incurs double the reliability penalty. BF16 and FP16 are interchangeable under binary scoring on single-GPU inference, which eliminates precision as a required control variable. The released D-study calculator enables practitioners to determine the number of items, models, and conditions needed for a target reliability before data collection.\n\nTwo extensions are planned. Multi-GPU tensor parallelism will test whether precision separability holds under distributed numerical non-associativity. Fine-tuning variance analysis will quantify how adaptation reshapes the item difficulty profiles that drive model$\\times$item interaction.",

  "old_string": "Existing studies have examined individual variance sources in isolation, each implicitly assuming separability. \\citet{sclar2024formatting} documented up to 76\\% accuracy swings from prompt formatting perturbations but held hardware and item selection fixed. BrittleBench \\citep{brittlebench2026} found 50\\% variance from prompt perturbation under the same constraint. \\citet{yuan2025layercast} showed that numerical precision and GPU type cause up to 9\\% accuracy variation but provided no factorial attribution to specific sources or their interactions. \\citet{mesting2026hidden} applied Generalizability theory (G-theory) to LLM evaluation and decomposed variance across five methodology facets on a single benchmark; judge identity dominated at 43.8\\%, but the study included no cross-benchmark comparison and excluded infrastructure facets such as numerical precision. Studies of answer ordering \\citep{pezeshkpour2024ordering} and hyperparameter sensitivity \\citep{dodge2020finetuning, henderson2018deep} have documented additional sources of variation, again in isolation from the factors above. Item Response Theory (IRT) approaches \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks, lalor2019irt} model item difficulty parameters to construct efficient benchmarks but do not account for prompt, seed, or ordering facets. \\citet{bouthillier2021accounting} established ANOVA-based variance decomposition as a principled template for ML reproducibility, but this work predates the LLM evaluation context where benchmark format introduces an additional structural variable. Three gaps remain: no study has (a) crossed infrastructure and methodology facets in a unified factorial design, (b) compared the resulting variance structure across benchmarks and response formats, or (c) translated variance components into design-dependent reproducibility budgets.",
  "new_string": "Existing studies have examined individual variance sources in isolation, each implicitly assuming separability. \\citet{sclar2024formatting} documented up to 76\\% accuracy swings from prompt formatting perturbations but held hardware and item selection fixed. BrittleBench \\citep{brittlebench2026} found 50\\% variance from prompt perturbation under the same constraint. \\citet{yuan2025layercast} showed that numerical precision and GPU type cause up to 9\\% accuracy variation but provided no factorial attribution to specific sources or their interactions. \\citet{madaan2024quantifying} proposed per-factor variance metrics for evaluation benchmarks but did not cross factors in a unified design. Studies of answer ordering \\citep{pezeshkpour2024ordering} and hyperparameter sensitivity \\citep{dodge2020finetuning, henderson2018deep} have documented additional sources of variation, again in isolation from the factors above. Item Response Theory (IRT) approaches \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks, lalor2019irt} model item difficulty parameters to construct efficient benchmarks but do not account for prompt, seed, or ordering facets; differential item functioning (DIF) across models is a known phenomenon in this literature \\citep{pezeshkpour2024ordering, zhou2026irt}. \\citet{bouthillier2021accounting} established ANOVA-based variance decomposition as a principled template for ML reproducibility, and G-theory has recently been applied to LLM-based writing assessment \\citep{wei2025gtheory}, but no study has applied it to benchmark evaluation where infrastructure and methodology facets interact. Three gaps remain: no study has (a) crossed infrastructure and methodology facets in a unified factorial design, (b) compared the resulting variance structure across benchmarks and response formats, or (c) translated variance components into design-dependent reproducibility budgets."

Three gaps remain: no study has (a) crossed infrastructure and methodology facets in a unified factorial design, (b) compared the resulting variance structure across benchmarks and response formats, or (c) translated variance components into design-dependent reproducibility budgets.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nOn multiple-choice benchmarks, item selection drives 34--38\\% of evaluation variance while prompt wording is negligible ($<$3\\%). On the free-form reasoning task GSM8K, the structure shifts: model$\\times$prompt interaction alone accounts for approximately 9\\%, while item selection drops to 17\\%. These numbers, drawn from our factorial analysis of 2.3 million evaluation records across five benchmarks, reveal that the variance structure of LLM evaluation is not a fixed property of the pipeline but shifts qualitatively with the response format. Current evaluation practice treats variance sources as independent nuisance factors and controls them one at a time: one study perturbs prompts, another varies precision, a third changes item subsets. This piecemeal approach cannot capture interactions between sources, nor can it predict how the relative importance of each source changes across evaluation settings. Leaderboard rankings built on single-condition evaluations are therefore sensitive to methodological choices that remain unreported and uncontrolled \\citep{rodriguez2021leaderboards, dehghani2021benchmark}. No unified factorial framework exists to decompose these sources simultaneously, quantify their interactions, and compare the resulting variance structure across benchmarks.\n\nExisting studies have examined individual variance sources in isolation, each implicitly assuming separability. \\citet{sclar2024formatting} documented up to 76\\% accuracy swings from prompt formatting perturbations but held hardware and item selection fixed. BrittleBench \\citep{brittlebench2026} found 50\\% variance from prompt perturbation under the same constraint. \\citet{yuan2025layercast} showed that numerical precision and GPU type cause up to 9\\% accuracy variation but provided no factorial attribution to specific sources or their interactions. \\citet{mesting2026hidden} applied Generalizability theory (G-theory) to LLM evaluation and decomposed variance across five methodology facets on a single benchmark; judge identity dominated at 43.8\\%, but the study included no cross-benchmark comparison and excluded infrastructure facets such as numerical precision. Studies of answer ordering \\citep{pezeshkpour2024ordering} and hyperparameter sensitivity \\citep{dodge2020finetuning, henderson2018deep} have documented additional sources of variation, again in isolation from the factors above. Item Response Theory (IRT) approaches \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks, lalor2019irt} model item difficulty parameters to construct efficient benchmarks but do not account for prompt, seed, or ordering facets. \\citet{bouthillier2021accounting} established ANOVA-based variance decomposition as a principled template for ML reproducibility, but this work predates the LLM evaluation context where benchmark format introduces an additional structural variable. Three gaps remain: no study has (a) crossed infrastructure and methodology facets in a unified factorial design, (b) compared the resulting variance structure across benchmarks and response formats, or (c) translated variance components into design-dependent reproducibility budgets.\n\nWe address these gaps with a multi-benchmark G-theory study \\citep{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability} that crosses seven facets (item, model, numerical precision, prompt template, random seed, sampling temperature, and answer ordering) across eight models and five benchmarks (MMLU, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag, GSM8K, and MATH), totaling 2.3 million records. The central finding is that variance structure is format-conditional. All three multiple-choice benchmarks share an item-dominated structure: item selection accounts for 34--38\\% of total variance, with model$\\times$item interaction as the second-largest source (30--36\\%). Free-form benchmarks (GSM8K, MATH) exhibit a different profile: item, model, model$\\times$item, and model$\\times$prompt each contribute 5--24\\%, with no single dominant source and larger residual variance (40--44\\%). The gap between MC item dominance (34--38\\%) and free-form item shares (17--24\\%) reflects a systematic format-dependent pattern in what drives evaluation variance. In multiple-choice settings, the item pool determines measurement reliability; in free-form settings, model identity and model$\\times$prompt interaction matter as much as item selection. A second finding is that model$\\times$item interaction (up to 36\\%) is the hidden second-largest variance source, larger than all methodology main effects combined. Models differ not in overall ability but in which specific items they find difficult. D-study projections translate these findings into practical design guidance: the format-conditional structure implies that reliability optimization strategies must differ between MC and free-form benchmarks. Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_benchmark} visualizes the contrast across all five benchmarks.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Format-conditional variance structure.} We show that the dominant variance source shifts between response formats: item selection dominates multiple-choice benchmarks (34--38\\%) while item, model, model$\\times$item, and model$\\times$prompt contribute 5--24\\% each in free-form generation. Model$\\times$item interaction (up to 36\\%) is the hidden second-largest source in cross-model analysis.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Precision separability.} Under binary scoring, BF16 and FP16 yield functionally identical results ($<$0.1\\% interaction with all other facets). This separability is metric-conditional: text-exact-match scoring reveals a 1.3\\% precision$\\times$temperature interaction absent under binary scoring.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Design-dependent reproducibility budgets.} Multi-facet evaluation designs require 10.0$\\times$ fewer items than single-condition designs to achieve $G \\geq 0.80$ (55 versus 552 items). IRT-selected 116-item subsets yield $G = 0.457$ under single-condition protocols, which confirms that item quality and evaluation design improve reliability through orthogonal mechanisms.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Open-source G-theory pipeline and D-study calculator.} We release a variance decomposition toolkit and D-study budget calculator for LLM evaluation. Practitioners can compute the number of items, models, and conditions needed for a target reliability before data collection.\n\\end{enumerate}",

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519/projects/nlp_variance_gtheory/docs/paper/introduction.tex",
  "old_string": "We address these gaps with a multi-benchmark G-theory study \\citep{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability} that crosses seven facets (item, model, numerical precision, prompt template, random seed, sampling temperature, and answer ordering) across eight models and five benchmarks (MMLU, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag, GSM8K, and MATH), totaling 2.3 million records. The central finding is that variance structure is format-conditional. All three multiple-choice benchmarks share an item-dominated structure: item selection accounts for 34--38\\% of total variance, with model$\\times$item interaction as the second-largest source (30--36\\%). Free-form benchmarks (GSM8K, MATH) exhibit a different profile: item, model, model$\\times$item, and model$\\times$prompt each contribute 5--24\\%, with no single dominant source and larger residual variance (40--44\\%). The gap between MC item dominance (34--38\\%) and free-form item shares (17--24\\%) reflects a systematic format-dependent pattern in what drives evaluation variance. In multiple-choice settings, the item pool determines measurement reliability; in free-form settings, model identity and model$\\times$prompt interaction matter as much as item selection. A second finding is that model$\\times$item interaction (up to 36\\%) is the hidden second-largest variance source, larger than all methodology main effects combined. Models differ not in overall ability but in which specific items they find difficult. D-study projections translate these findings into practical design guidance: the format-conditional structure implies that reliability optimization strategies must differ between MC and free-form benchmarks. Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_benchmark} visualizes the contrast across all five benchmarks.",
  "new_string": "We address these gaps with a multi-benchmark G-theory study \\citep{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability} that crosses seven facets (item, model, numerical precision, prompt template, random seed, sampling temperature, and answer ordering) across eight open-weight 7--9B parameter models and five benchmarks (MMLU, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag, GSM8K, and MATH), totaling 2.3 million records. Our results suggest that the variance structure of LLM evaluation is format-associated. We define \\emph{item-related variance} as the sum of item difficulty and model$\\times$item interaction (differential item functioning, DIF; \\S\\ref{sec:gtheory}). Across all three multiple-choice benchmarks, item-related variance concentrates at 68--72\\%, while on free-form benchmarks (GSM8K, MATH) it disperses to 31--41\\%, with no overlap across all five benchmarks. Model$\\times$item interaction (up to 36\\%) is the second-largest variance source in our multi-facet decomposition, consistent with prior IRT findings on differential item functioning \\citep{pezeshkpour2024ordering, polo2024tinybenchmarks}. Models differ not in overall ability but in which specific items they find difficult. This format-associated pattern implies distinct optimization strategies: for MC benchmarks, item pool composition is the primary reliability lever; for free-form benchmarks, model identity and model$\\times$prompt interaction matter as much as item selection. D-study projections reveal that multi-facet designs require 11--22$\\times$ fewer items than single-condition designs to achieve $G \\geq 0.80$, with the efficiency gain itself being format-associated (MC: 11--12$\\times$; FF: 21--22$\\times$). Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_benchmark} visualizes the contrast across all five benchmarks."
}

Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_benchmark} visualizes the contrast across all five benchmarks.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nOn multiple-choice benchmarks, item selection drives 34--38\\% of evaluation variance while prompt wording is negligible ($<$3\\%). On the free-form reasoning task GSM8K, the structure shifts: model$\\times$prompt interaction alone accounts for approximately 9\\%, while item selection drops to 17\\%. These numbers, drawn from our factorial analysis of 2.3 million evaluation records across five benchmarks, reveal that the variance structure of LLM evaluation is not a fixed property of the pipeline but shifts qualitatively with the response format. Current evaluation practice treats variance sources as independent nuisance factors and controls them one at a time: one study perturbs prompts, another varies precision, a third changes item subsets. This piecemeal approach cannot capture interactions between sources, nor can it predict how the relative importance of each source changes across evaluation settings. Leaderboard rankings built on single-condition evaluations are therefore sensitive to methodological choices that remain unreported and uncontrolled \\citep{rodriguez2021leaderboards, dehghani2021benchmark}. No unified factorial framework exists to decompose these sources simultaneously, quantify their interactions, and compare the resulting variance structure across benchmarks.\n\nExisting studies have examined individual variance sources in isolation, each implicitly assuming separability. \\citet{sclar2024formatting} documented up to 76\\% accuracy swings from prompt formatting perturbations but held hardware and item selection fixed. BrittleBench \\citep{brittlebench2026} found 50\\% variance from prompt perturbation under the same constraint. \\citet{yuan2025layercast} showed that numerical precision and GPU type cause up to 9\\% accuracy variation but provided no factorial attribution to specific sources or their interactions. \\citet{madaan2024quantifying} proposed per-factor variance metrics for evaluation benchmarks but did not cross factors in a unified design. Studies of answer ordering \\citep{pezeshkpour2024ordering} and hyperparameter sensitivity \\citep{dodge2020finetuning, henderson2018deep} have documented additional sources of variation, again in isolation from the factors above. Item Response Theory (IRT) approaches \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks, lalor2019irt} model item difficulty parameters to construct efficient benchmarks but do not account for prompt, seed, or ordering facets; differential item functioning (DIF) across models is a known phenomenon in this literature \\citep{pezeshkpour2024ordering, zhou2026irt}. \\citet{bouthillier2021accounting} established ANOVA-based variance decomposition as a principled template for ML reproducibility, and G-theory has recently been applied to LLM-based writing assessment \\citep{wei2025gtheory}, but no study has applied it to benchmark evaluation where infrastructure and methodology facets interact. Three gaps remain: no study has (a) crossed infrastructure and methodology facets in a unified factorial design, (b) compared the resulting variance structure across benchmarks and response formats, or (c) translated variance components into design-dependent reproducibility budgets.\n\nWe address these gaps with a multi-benchmark G-theory study \\citep{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability} that crosses seven facets (item, model, numerical precision, prompt template, random seed, sampling temperature, and answer ordering) across eight models and five benchmarks (MMLU, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag, GSM8K, and MATH), totaling 2.3 million records. The central finding is that variance structure is format-conditional. All three multiple-choice benchmarks share an item-dominated structure: item selection accounts for 34--38\\% of total variance, with model$\\times$item interaction as the second-largest source (30--36\\%). Free-form benchmarks (GSM8K, MATH) exhibit a different profile: item, model, model$\\times$item, and model$\\times$prompt each contribute 5--24\\%, with no single dominant source and larger residual variance (40--44\\%). The gap between MC item dominance (34--38\\%) and free-form item shares (17--24\\%) reflects a systematic format-dependent pattern in what drives evaluation variance. In multiple-choice settings, the item pool determines measurement reliability; in free-form settings, model identity and model$\\times$prompt interaction matter as much as item selection. A second finding is that model$\\times$item interaction (up to 36\\%) is the hidden second-largest variance source, larger than all methodology main effects combined. Models differ not in overall ability but in which specific items they find difficult. D-study projections translate these findings into practical design guidance: the format-conditional structure implies that reliability optimization strategies must differ between MC and free-form benchmarks. Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_benchmark} visualizes the contrast across all five benchmarks.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Format-conditional variance structure.} We show that the dominant variance source shifts between response formats: item selection dominates multiple-choice benchmarks (34--38\\%) while item, model, model$\\times$item, and model$\\times$prompt contribute 5--24\\% each in free-form generation. Model$\\times$item interaction (up to 36\\%) is the hidden second-largest source in cross-model analysis.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Precision separability.} Under binary scoring, BF16 and FP16 yield functionally identical results ($<$0.1\\% interaction with all other facets). This separability is metric-conditional: text-exact-match scoring reveals a 1.3\\% precision$\\times$temperature interaction absent under binary scoring.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Design-dependent reproducibility budgets.} Multi-facet evaluation designs require 10.0$\\times$ fewer items than single-condition designs to achieve $G \\geq 0.80$ (55 versus 552 items). IRT-selected 116-item subsets yield $G = 0.457$ under single-condition protocols, which confirms that item quality and evaluation design improve reliability through orthogonal mechanisms.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Open-source G-theory pipeline and D-study calculator.} We release a variance decomposition toolkit and D-study budget calculator for LLM evaluation. Practitioners can compute the number of items, models, and conditions needed for a target reliability before data collection.\n\\end{enumerate}",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\\label{sec:introduction}\n\nOn multiple-choice benchmarks, item selection drives 34--38\\% of evaluation variance while prompt wording is negligible ($<$3\\%). On the free-form reasoning task GSM8K, the structure shifts: model$\\times$prompt interaction alone accounts for approximately 9\\%, while item selection drops to 17\\%. These numbers, drawn from our factorial analysis of 2.3 million evaluation records across five benchmarks, reveal that the variance structure of LLM evaluation is not a fixed property of the pipeline but shifts qualitatively with the response format. Current evaluation practice treats variance sources as independent nuisance factors and controls them one at a time: one study perturbs prompts, another varies precision, a third changes item subsets. This piecemeal approach cannot capture interactions between sources, nor can it predict how the relative importance of each source changes across evaluation settings. Leaderboard rankings built on single-condition evaluations are therefore sensitive to methodological choices that remain unreported and uncontrolled \\citep{rodriguez2021leaderboards, dehghani2021benchmark}. No unified factorial framework exists to decompose these sources simultaneously, quantify their interactions, and compare the resulting variance structure across benchmarks.\n\nExisting studies have examined individual variance sources in isolation, each implicitly assuming separability. \\citet{sclar2024formatting} documented up to 76\\% accuracy swings from prompt formatting perturbations but held hardware and item selection fixed. BrittleBench \\citep{brittlebench2026} found 50\\% variance from prompt perturbation under the same constraint. \\citet{yuan2025layercast} showed that numerical precision and GPU type cause up to 9\\% accuracy variation but provided no factorial attribution to specific sources or their interactions. \\citet{madaan2024quantifying} proposed per-factor variance metrics for evaluation benchmarks but did not cross factors in a unified design. Studies of answer ordering \\citep{pezeshkpour2024ordering} and hyperparameter sensitivity \\citep{dodge2020finetuning, henderson2018deep} have documented additional sources of variation, again in isolation from the factors above. Item Response Theory (IRT) approaches \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks, lalor2019irt} model item difficulty parameters to construct efficient benchmarks but do not account for prompt, seed, or ordering facets; differential item functioning (DIF) across models is a known phenomenon in this literature \\citep{pezeshkpour2024ordering, zhou2026irt}. \\citet{bouthillier2021accounting} established ANOVA-based variance decomposition as a principled template for ML reproducibility, and G-theory has recently been applied to LLM-based writing assessment \\citep{wei2025gtheory}, but no study has applied it to benchmark evaluation where infrastructure and methodology facets interact. Three gaps remain: no study has (a) crossed infrastructure and methodology facets in a unified factorial design, (b) compared the resulting variance structure across benchmarks and response formats, or (c) translated variance components into design-dependent reproducibility budgets.\n\nWe address these gaps with a multi-benchmark G-theory study \\citep{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability} that crosses seven facets (item, model, numerical precision, prompt template, random seed, sampling temperature, and answer ordering) across eight open-weight 7--9B parameter models and five benchmarks (MMLU, ARC-Challenge, HellaSwag, GSM8K, and MATH), totaling 2.3 million records. Our results suggest that the variance structure of LLM evaluation is format-associated. We define \\emph{item-related variance} as the sum of item difficulty and model$\\times$item interaction (differential item functioning, DIF; \\S\\ref{sec:gtheory}). Across all three multiple-choice benchmarks, item-related variance concentrates at 68--72\\%, while on free-form benchmarks (GSM8K, MATH) it disperses to 31--41\\%, with no overlap across all five benchmarks. Model$\\times$item interaction (up to 36\\%) is the second-largest variance source in our multi-facet decomposition, consistent with prior IRT findings on differential item functioning \\citep{pezeshkpour2024ordering, polo2024tinybenchmarks}. Models differ not in overall ability but in which specific items they find difficult. This format-associated pattern implies distinct optimization strategies: for MC benchmarks, item pool composition is the primary reliability lever; for free-form benchmarks, model identity and model$\\times$prompt interaction matter as much as item selection. D-study projections reveal that multi-facet designs require 11--22$\\times$ fewer items than single-condition designs to achieve $G \\geq 0.80$, with the efficiency gain itself being format-associated (MC: 11--12$\\times$; FF: 21--22$\\times$). Figure~\\ref{fig:cross_benchmark} visualizes the contrast across all five benchmarks.\n\nOur contributions are as follows:\n\\begin{enumerate}\n    \\item \\textbf{Format-conditional variance structure.} We show that the dominant variance source shifts between response formats: item selection dominates multiple-choice benchmarks (34--38\\%) while item, model, model$\\times$item, and model$\\times$prompt contribute 5--24\\% each in free-form generation. Model$\\times$item interaction (up to 36\\%) is the hidden second-largest source in cross-model analysis.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Precision separability.} Under binary scoring, BF16 and FP16 yield functionally identical results ($<$0.1\\% interaction with all other facets). This separability is metric-conditional: text-exact-match scoring reveals a 1.3\\% precision$\\times$temperature interaction absent under binary scoring.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Design-dependent reproducibility budgets.} Multi-facet evaluation designs require 10.0$\\times$ fewer items than single-condition designs to achieve $G \\geq 0.80$ (55 versus 552 items). IRT-selected 116-item subsets yield $G = 0.457$ under single-condition protocols, which confirms that item quality and evaluation design improve reliability through orthogonal mechanisms.\n\n    \\item \\textbf{Open-source G-theory pipeline and D-study calculator.} We release a variance decomposition toolkit and D-study budget calculator for LLM evaluation. Practitioners can compute the number of items, models, and conditions needed for a target reliability before data collection.\n\\end{enumerate}",

  "old_string": "Factorial decomposition of evaluation variance has roots in the ML reproducibility literature. \\citet{bouthillier2021accounting} introduced an ANOVA-based template that decomposes benchmark variance across implementation details, hyperparameters, and data splits, establishing that design choices account for a substantial share of reproducibility failures. Several subsequent studies applied similar decomposition logic to specific facets of model evaluation \\citep{dodge2020finetuning, henderson2018deep, magnusson2023reproducibility}. \\citet{mesting2026hidden} brought Generalizability theory into LLM evaluation, decomposing variance across five methodology facets on a single benchmark; judge identity dominated at 43.8\\%, with prompt$\\times$judge interaction at 12.4\\%. However, this study used a single benchmark, included no infrastructure facets such as numerical precision, and fixed the judge as an LLM evaluator rather than isolating other sources. \\citet{kunievsky2025intent} proposed an alternative decomposition that separates intent uncertainty, articulation variance, and model uncertainty, but this framework does not cross infrastructure and methodology facets in a factorial design. Our work extends these approaches by crossing seven facets across five benchmarks, enabling direct comparison of variance structures across evaluation formats.",
  "new_string": "Factorial decomposition of evaluation variance has roots in the ML reproducibility literature. \\citet{bouthillier2021accounting} introduced an ANOVA-based template that decomposes benchmark variance across implementation details, hyperparameters, and data splits, establishing that design choices account for a substantial share of reproducibility failures. Several subsequent studies applied similar decomposition logic to specific facets of model evaluation \\citep{dodge2020finetuning, henderson2018deep, magnusson2023reproducibility}. \\citet{madaan2024quantifying} proposed per-factor variance metrics for LLM evaluation benchmarks but did not cross factors simultaneously. Recently, G-theory has been applied to LLM-based writing assessment \\citep{wei2025gtheory}, but this work addresses educational scoring consistency rather than benchmark evaluation variance where infrastructure and methodology facets interact. \\citet{kunievsky2025intent} proposed an alternative decomposition that separates intent uncertainty, articulation variance, and model uncertainty, but this framework does not cross infrastructure and methodology facets in a factorial design. Our work is, to our knowledge, the first to apply G-theory to LLM benchmark evaluation, crossing seven facets across five benchmarks to enable direct comparison of variance structures across evaluation formats."
}

Several subsequent studies applied similar decomposition logic to specific facets of model evaluation \\citep{dodge2020finetuning, henderson2018deep, magnusson2023reproducibility}. \\citet{mesting2026hidden} brought Generalizability theory into LLM evaluation, decomposing variance across five methodology facets on a single benchmark; judge identity dominated at 43.8\\%, with prompt$\\times$judge interaction at 12.4\\%. However, this study used a single benchmark, included no infrastructure facets such as numerical precision, and fixed the judge as an LLM evaluator rather than isolating other sources. \\citet{kunievsky2025intent} proposed an alternative decomposition that separates intent uncertainty, articulation variance, and model uncertainty, but this framework does not cross infrastructure and methodology facets in a factorial design. Our work extends these approaches by crossing seven facets across five benchmarks, enabling direct comparison of variance structures across evaluation formats.",
    "newString": "Factorial decomposition of evaluation variance has roots in the ML reproducibility literature. \\citet{bouthillier2021accounting} introduced an ANOVA-based template that decomposes benchmark variance across implementation details, hyperparameters, and data splits, establishing that design choices account for a substantial share of reproducibility failures. Several subsequent studies applied similar decomposition logic to specific facets of model evaluation \\citep{dodge2020finetuning, henderson2018deep, magnusson2023reproducibility}. \\citet{madaan2024quantifying} proposed per-factor variance metrics for LLM evaluation benchmarks but did not cross factors simultaneously. Recently, G-theory has been applied to LLM-based writing assessment \\citep{wei2025gtheory}, but this work addresses educational scoring consistency rather than benchmark evaluation variance where infrastructure and methodology facets interact. \\citet{kunievsky2025intent} proposed an alternative decomposition that separates intent uncertainty, articulation variance, and model uncertainty, but this framework does not cross infrastructure and methodology facets in a factorial design. Our work is, to our knowledge, the first to apply G-theory to LLM benchmark evaluation, crossing seven facets across five benchmarks to enable direct comparison of variance structures across evaluation formats.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related}\n\n\\paragraph{Variance Decomposition in ML/NLP Evaluation.}\nFactorial decomposition of evaluation variance has roots in the ML reproducibility literature. \\citet{bouthillier2021accounting} introduced an ANOVA-based template that decomposes benchmark variance across implementation details, hyperparameters, and data splits, establishing that design choices account for a substantial share of reproducibility failures. Several subsequent studies applied similar decomposition logic to specific facets of model evaluation \\citep{dodge2020finetuning, henderson2018deep, magnusson2023reproducibility}. \\citet{mesting2026hidden} brought Generalizability theory into LLM evaluation, decomposing variance across five methodology facets on a single benchmark; judge identity dominated at 43.8\\%, with prompt$\\times$judge interaction at 12.4\\%. However, this study used a single benchmark, included no infrastructure facets such as numerical precision, and fixed the judge as an LLM evaluator rather than isolating other sources. \\citet{kunievsky2025intent} proposed an alternative decomposition that separates intent uncertainty, articulation variance, and model uncertainty, but this framework does not cross infrastructure and methodology facets in a factorial design. Our work extends these approaches by crossing seven facets across five benchmarks, enabling direct comparison of variance structures across evaluation formats.\n\n\\paragraph{Infrastructure and Prompt Variance.}\nA parallel line of work has examined individual variance sources in isolation. On the infrastructure side, \\citet{yuan2025layercast} documented up to 9\\% accuracy variation from numerical precision and GPU type but provided no factorial attribution. \\citet{messina2026background} formalized the concept of background temperature from floating-point non-associativity in GPU computation, and \\citet{thinkingmachines2025batch} proposed batch-invariant kernels to mitigate CUDA nondeterminism. On the methodology side, \\citet{sclar2024formatting} found up to 76\\% accuracy swings from prompt formatting changes on fixed hardware, and BrittleBench \\citep{brittlebench2026} reported 50\\% variance from prompt perturbation. \\citet{pezeshkpour2024ordering} showed that answer ordering in multiple-choice benchmarks introduces systematic bias, while \\citet{bui2025seeds} and \\citet{biderman2024lessons} documented sensitivity to random seeds and training configurations. Each of these studies isolates one factor while holding others fixed, which precludes estimating interactions between sources or comparing their relative magnitudes within a unified design.\n\n\\paragraph{IRT and Efficient Benchmarking.}\nItem Response Theory (IRT) offers a complementary perspective on evaluation design by modeling item difficulty and discrimination parameters. \\citet{lalor2019irt} introduced IRT-based item analysis for NLP benchmarks, and \\citet{rodriguez2021leaderboards} showed that IRT reveals hidden properties of evaluation items that raw accuracy scores obscure. Building on this foundation, \\citet{polo2024tinybenchmarks} developed tinyBenchmarks, using IRT-based subsampling to reduce evaluation cost while preserving ranking accuracy, and \\citet{zhou2026irt} recommended a 116-item subset via PSN-IRT for efficient MMLU evaluation. \\citet{perlitz2023efficient} explored related strategies for benchmark compression. These approaches optimize item selection for latent trait estimation but do not model variance from prompt, seed, or ordering facets. IRT-selected items evaluated under a single methodological condition can therefore systematically underestimate the number of items required for reliable measurement across diverse evaluation settings.\n\n\\paragraph{Binary Data and Psychometrics Foundations.}\nOur application of G-theory to binary correctness scores draws on established psychometric methodology. \\citet{shavelson1991generalizability} and \\citet{brennan2001generalizability} provide the canonical framework for multi-facet reliability analysis. \\citet{mushquash2006gtheory} extended G-theory to binary data, and \\citet{jiang2018bayesian} developed logistic-link generalized G-theory for binary outcomes. \\citet{tuerlinckx2006glmm} established the connection between generalized linear mixed models and G-theory variance components. These methods ground our use of Henderson Method~I for binary correctness scores (\\S\\ref{sec:binary}).\n\n\\paragraph{Positioning.}\nRelative to \\citet{mesting2026hidden}, we fix the judge (generation-based scoring) to isolate the remaining facets; their judge-dominated variance (43.8\\%) would otherwise mask all other sources. Our multi-benchmark design reveals format-conditional structure that single-benchmark studies cannot detect. Relative to IRT approaches \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks, zhou2026irt}, G-theory and IRT optimize orthogonal dimensions of evaluation design: IRT selects informative items, while G-theory determines the conditions needed for a target reliability. Our D-study quantifies this complementarity: multi-facet designs require 10.0$\\times$ fewer items than single-condition protocols for equivalent reliability. Relative to isolated-factor studies \\citep{yuan2025layercast, sclar2024formatting, brittlebench2026}, our design crosses infrastructure and methodology facets within a single factorial framework, enabling interaction estimation that per-factor studies cannot provide.",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 2,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 2,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [

Several subsequent studies applied similar decomposition logic to specific facets of model evaluation \\citep{dodge2020finetuning, henderson2018deep, magnusson2023reproducibility}. \\citet{mesting2026hidden} brought Generalizability theory into LLM evaluation, decomposing variance across five methodology facets on a single benchmark; judge identity dominated at 43.8\\%, with prompt$\\times$judge interaction at 12.4\\%. However, this study used a single benchmark, included no infrastructure facets such as numerical precision, and fixed the judge as an LLM evaluator rather than isolating other sources. \\citet{kunievsky2025intent} proposed an alternative decomposition that separates intent uncertainty, articulation variance, and model uncertainty, but this framework does not cross infrastructure and methodology facets in a factorial design.   "old_string": "\\paragraph{Positioning.}\nRelative to \\citet{mesting2026hidden}, we fix the judge (generation-based scoring) to isolate the remaining facets; their judge-dominated variance (43.8\\%) would otherwise mask all other sources. Our multi-benchmark design reveals format-conditional structure that single-benchmark studies cannot detect. Relative to IRT approaches \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks, zhou2026irt}, G-theory and IRT optimize orthogonal dimensions of evaluation design: IRT selects informative items, while G-theory determines the conditions needed for a target reliability. Our D-study quantifies this complementarity: multi-facet designs require 10.0$\\times$ fewer items than single-condition protocols for equivalent reliability. Relative to isolated-factor studies \\citep{yuan2025layercast, sclar2024formatting, brittlebench2026}, our design crosses infrastructure and methodology facets within a single factorial framework, enabling interaction estimation that per-factor studies cannot provide.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{Positioning.}\nRelative to \\citet{bouthillier2021accounting}, who established ANOVA-based variance decomposition for ML benchmarks, we adopt G-theory's D-study framework, which enables prospective design optimization---predicting the number of items and conditions needed for a target reliability \\emph{before} data collection. Our multi-benchmark design reveals format-associated structure that single-benchmark studies cannot detect. Relative to IRT approaches \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks, zhou2026irt}, G-theory and IRT optimize orthogonal dimensions of evaluation design: IRT selects informative items, while G-theory determines the conditions needed for a target reliability. This is an illustrative comparison across different optimization objectives---IRT optimizes item discrimination, G-theory optimizes cross-facet generalizability. Relative to isolated-factor studies \\citep{yuan2025layercast, sclar2024formatting, brittlebench2026, madaan2024quantifying}, our design crosses infrastructure and methodology facets within a single factorial framework, enabling interaction estimation that per-factor studies cannot provide."
}

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_018ddDzSukvteD9aqr3NgFZR`</summary>

```text
122	GSM8K & 128 & [103, 162] & 2768 & [2230, 3531] & 21.6 [20.7, 23.0] \\
123	MATH & 101 & [86, 122] & 2160 & [1853, 2631] & 21.4 [20.2, 22.5] \\
124	\bottomrule
125	\end{tabular}
126	
127	\vspace{2pt}
128	{\scriptsize MC mean ratio: 12.0$\times$ [11.6, 12.5]; FF mean ratio: 21.5$\times$ [20.4, 22.8].}
129	\end{table}
130	
131	The comparison with IRT-based efficient benchmarking is instructive. \citet{zhou2026irt} use IRT to select 116 informative items from MMLU, optimizing for item discrimination. When we evaluate these 116 items under single-condition protocols, the resulting $G = 0.457$ falls well below the reliability threshold. This is not a failure of IRT item selection; rather, it reflects that IRT optimizes a different objective (latent trait estimation) that does not account for methodological facet variance. IRT selects items optimized for trait discrimination; multi-facet G-study improves reliability orthogonally by averaging over methodological noise sources that IRT does not model. Under the multi-facet design, 116 items also exceed $G = 0.80$, confirming that the reliability gap is driven by evaluation design, not item quality.
132	
133	The efficiency gap between multi-facet and single-condition designs is itself format-conditional. Per-benchmark D-study projections reveal that MC benchmarks exhibit a 10--12$\times$ ratio (MMLU 10.0$\times$, ARC 11.0$\times$, HellaSwag 11.9$\times$), while FF benchmarks show a 21--22$\times$ ratio (GSM8K 21.6$\times$, MATH 21.4$\times$). Single-condition evaluation of free-form benchmarks incurs approximately double the reliability penalty of multiple-choice benchmarks, making multi-facet designs even more critical for reasoning tasks where variance distributes across model, prompt, and interaction components (\S\ref{sec:format_conditional}).
134	
135	Figure~\ref{fig:dstudy} plots the D-study projection curves for each design. The multi-facet curve saturates rapidly (diminishing returns beyond $\sim$150 items), while the single-condition curve rises slowly and does not reach $G = 0.80$ within typical benchmark sizes.
136	
137	\begin{figure}[t]
138	\centering
139	\includegraphics[width=\columnwidth]{fig3_dstudy_curves}
140	\caption{D-study projection curves for three evaluation designs. The multi-facet design (10.0$\times$ fewer items) reaches $G \geq 0.80$ at 55 items; the single-condition design requires 552 items. The shaded band shows 95\% bootstrap CIs.}
141	\label{fig:dstudy}
