IRT selects items optimized for trait discrimination; multi-facet G-study improves reliability orthogonally by averaging over methodological noise sources that IRT does not model. Under the multi-facet design, 116 items also exceed $G = 0.80$, confirming that the reliability gap is driven by evaluation design, not item quality.\n\nThe efficiency gap between multi-facet and single-condition designs is itself format-conditional. Per-benchmark D-study projections reveal that MC benchmarks exhibit a 10--12$\\times$ ratio (MMLU 10.0$\\times$, ARC 11.0$\\times$, HellaSwag 11.9$\\times$), while FF benchmarks show a 21--22$\\times$ ratio (GSM8K 21.6$\\times$, MATH 21.4$\\times$). Single-condition evaluation of free-form benchmarks incurs approximately double the reliability penalty of multiple-choice benchmarks, making multi-facet designs even more critical for reasoning tasks where variance distributes across model, prompt, and interaction components (\\S\\ref{sec:format_conditional}).\n\nFigure~\\ref{fig:dstudy} plots the D-study projection curves for each design. The multi-facet curve saturates rapidly (diminishing returns beyond $\\sim$150 items), while the single-condition curve rises slowly and does not reach $G = 0.80$ within typical benchmark sizes.\n\n\\begin{figure}[t]\n\\centering\n\\includegraphics[width=\\columnwidth]{fig3_dstudy_curves}\n\\caption{D-study projection curves for three evaluation designs. The multi-facet design (10.0$\\times$ fewer items) reaches $G \\geq 0.80$ at 55 items; the single-condition design requires 552 items. The shaded band shows 95\\% bootstrap CIs.}\n\\label{fig:dstudy}",
      "numLines": 20,
      "startLine": 122,
      "totalLines": 147
    }
  },

```text
11	\paragraph{Results.} Under binary scoring, item difficulty (\texttt{item\_id}) accounts for 58.4\% of total variance, with the residual term contributing 23.1\%. All main facet effects are negligible ($<$1\% each): precision 0.9\%, prompt template, temperature, seed, and ordering each $<$0.5\%. The interesting structure appears in item-conditional interactions: prompt$\times$item is the largest at 5.5\%, followed by seed$\times$item (4.7\%), precision$\times$item (3.3\%), temperature$\times$item (2.9\%), and ordering$\times$item (0.8\%). This pattern indicates that methodological choices do not shift performance uniformly across items; instead, specific items are differentially sensitive to specific facets---prompt template variation produces the largest item-conditional effect.
12	
13	The resulting generalizability coefficient is $G_\text{item} = 0.936$, indicating that 200 items under multi-facet conditions produce highly reliable rankings. A D-study projection shows $G \geq 0.80$ is achievable with as few as 55 items in this multi-facet design.
14	
15	For text-exact-match scoring (requiring verbatim string matches), the variance structure shifts dramatically: temperature dominates at 79.7\% of variance, and $G_\text{item}$ collapses to 0.007. This metric-conditional divergence motivates our exclusive use of binary correctness for the remaining analyses.
```

</details>

### Tool result

_source line 2842 · 2026-05-22T03:53:29.110Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01541yoRphiAp3fwTcDxmV3i`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	This study suggests that the variance structure of LLM evaluation is format-associated. Across three multiple-choice benchmarks, item-related variance---item difficulty plus model$\times$item differential item functioning (DIF)---concentrates at 68--72\%, while on free-form benchmarks (GSM8K, MATH) it disperses to 31--41\%, with no overlap across all five benchmarks. Model$\times$item interaction (up to 36\%) is the second-largest source in cross-model analysis, consistent with DIF patterns identified in IRT research: models differ in which items they find difficult, not in overall ability. Our work introduces G-theory as a multi-facet variance decomposition framework for LLM benchmark evaluation, enabling format-associated comparison and actionable design budgets.
5	
6	The format-conditional structure implies distinct optimization strategies. For multiple-choice benchmarks, item pool composition is the primary reliability lever; varying prompts or seeds contributes negligible variance. For free-form benchmarks, model identity and model$\times$prompt interaction matter as much as item selection, and evaluation protocols should systematically vary both. The multi-facet efficiency gain is itself format-conditional: MC benchmarks show a 10--12$\times$ ratio while FF benchmarks show 21--22$\times$, indicating that single-condition evaluation of reasoning tasks incurs double the reliability penalty. BF16 and FP16 are interchangeable under binary scoring on single-GPU inference, which eliminates precision as a required control variable. The released D-study calculator enables practitioners to determine the number of items, models, and conditions needed for a target reliability before data collection.
7	
8	Two extensions are planned. Multi-GPU tensor parallelism will test whether precision separability holds under distributed numerical non-associativity. Fine-tuning variance analysis will quantify how adaptation reshapes the item difficulty profiles that drive model$\times$item interaction.
```

</details>

    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nThis study suggests that the variance structure of LLM evaluation is format-associated. Across three multiple-choice benchmarks, item-related variance---item difficulty plus model$\\times$item differential item functioning (DIF)---concentrates at 68--72\\%, while on free-form benchmarks (GSM8K, MATH) it disperses to 31--41\\%, with no overlap across all five benchmarks. Model$\\times$item interaction (up to 36\\%) is the second-largest source in cross-model analysis, consistent with DIF patterns identified in IRT research: models differ in which items they find difficult, not in overall ability. Our work introduces G-theory as a multi-facet variance decomposition framework for LLM benchmark evaluation, enabling format-associated comparison and actionable design budgets.\n\nThe format-conditional structure implies distinct optimization strategies. For multiple-choice benchmarks, item pool composition is the primary reliability lever; varying prompts or seeds contributes negligible variance. For free-form benchmarks, model identity and model$\\times$prompt interaction matter as much as item selection, and evaluation protocols should systematically vary both. The multi-facet efficiency gain is itself format-conditional: MC benchmarks show a 10--12$\\times$ ratio while FF benchmarks show 21--22$\\times$, indicating that single-condition evaluation of reasoning tasks incurs double the reliability penalty. BF16 and FP16 are interchangeable under binary scoring on single-GPU inference, which eliminates precision as a required control variable. The released D-study calculator enables practitioners to determine the number of items, models, and conditions needed for a target reliability before data collection.\n\nTwo extensions are planned. Multi-GPU tensor parallelism will test whether precision separability holds under distributed numerical non-associativity. Fine-tuning variance analysis will quantify how adaptation reshapes the item difficulty profiles that drive model$\\times$item interaction.",

{
  "replace_all": false,
  "file_path": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0519/projects/nlp_variance_gtheory/docs/paper/limitations.tex",
  "old_string": "Our findings are bounded by three scope constraints. First, the model sample comprises eight open-weight 7--9B parameter models; the results do not generalize to API-based models, larger models, or fine-tuned variants. Second, all experiments run on single-GPU inference (NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000); multi-GPU tensor parallelism introduces additional numerical non-associativity that our design does not capture. Third, the benchmark sample covers three multiple-choice and two free-form tasks; other evaluation formats such as code generation, long-form text production, and multi-turn dialogue may exhibit different variance structures.",
  "new_string": "Our findings are bounded by three scope constraints. First, the model sample comprises eight open-weight 7--9B parameter instruction-tuned models; the results do not generalize to API-based models (where infrastructure is opaque), base (non-instruction-tuned) models, models below 7B or above 9B parameters, or domain-adapted fine-tuned variants. Variance structure may be scale-dependent: larger models with higher accuracy could exhibit compressed item variance due to ceiling effects, while smaller models may show different interaction patterns. Second, all experiments run on single-GPU inference (NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000); multi-GPU tensor parallelism introduces additional numerical non-associativity that our design does not capture. Third, the benchmark sample covers three multiple-choice and two free-form tasks; other evaluation formats such as code generation, long-form text production, and multi-turn dialogue may exhibit different variance structures."
}

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01BLGmpoLNU2KY5aqsEFBZyE`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Related Work}
2	\label{sec:related}
3	
4	\paragraph{Variance Decomposition in ML/NLP Evaluation.}
5	Factorial decomposition of evaluation variance has roots in the ML reproducibility literature. \citet{bouthillier2021accounting} introduced an ANOVA-based template that decomposes benchmark variance across implementation details, hyperparameters, and data splits, establishing that design choices account for a substantial share of reproducibility failures. Several subsequent studies applied similar decomposition logic to specific facets of model evaluation \citep{dodge2020finetuning, henderson2018deep, magnusson2023reproducibility}. \citet{madaan2024quantifying} proposed per-factor variance metrics for LLM evaluation benchmarks but did not cross factors simultaneously. Recently, G-theory has been applied to LLM-based writing assessment \citep{wei2025gtheory}, but this work addresses educational scoring consistency rather than benchmark evaluation variance where infrastructure and methodology facets interact. \citet{kunievsky2025intent} proposed an alternative decomposition that separates intent uncertainty, articulation variance, and model uncertainty, but this framework does not cross infrastructure and methodology facets in a factorial design. Our work is, to our knowledge, the first to apply G-theory to LLM benchmark evaluation, crossing seven facets across five benchmarks to enable direct comparison of variance structures across evaluation formats.
6	
7	\paragraph{Infrastructure and Prompt Variance.}
8	A parallel line of work has examined individual variance sources in isolation. On the infrastructure side, \citet{yuan2025layercast} documented up to 9\% accuracy variation from numerical precision and GPU type but provided no factorial attribution. \citet{messina2026background} formalized the concept of background temperature from floating-point non-associativity in GPU computation, and \citet{thinkingmachines2025batch} proposed batch-invariant kernels to mitigate CUDA nondeterminism. On the methodology side, \citet{sclar2024formatting} found up to 76\% accuracy swings from prompt formatting changes on fixed hardware, and BrittleBench \citep{brittlebench2026} reported 50\% variance from prompt perturbation. \citet{pezeshkpour2024ordering} showed that answer ordering in multiple-choice benchmarks introduces systematic bias, while \citet{bui2025seeds} and \citet{biderman2024lessons} documented sensitivity to random seeds and training configurations. Each of these studies isolates one factor while holding others fixed, which precludes estimating interactions between sources or comparing their relative magnitudes within a unified design.
9	
10	\paragraph{IRT and Efficient Benchmarking.}
11	Item Response Theory (IRT) offers a complementary perspective on evaluation design by modeling item difficulty and discrimination parameters. \citet{lalor2019irt} introduced IRT-based item analysis for NLP benchmarks, and \citet{rodriguez2021leaderboards} showed that IRT reveals hidden properties of evaluation items that raw accuracy scores obscure. Building on this foundation, \citet{polo2024tinybenchmarks} developed tinyBenchmarks, using IRT-based subsampling to reduce evaluation cost while preserving ranking accuracy, and \citet{zhou2026irt} recommended a 116-item subset via PSN-IRT for efficient MMLU evaluation. \citet{perlitz2023efficient} explored related strategies for benchmark compression. These approaches optimize item selection for latent trait estimation but do not model variance from prompt, seed, or ordering facets. IRT-selected items evaluated under a single methodological condition can therefore systematically underestimate the number of items required for reliable measurement across diverse evaluation settings.
12	
13	\paragraph{Binary Data and Psychometrics Foundations.}
14	Our application of G-theory to binary correctness scores draws on established psychometric methodology. \citet{shavelson1991generalizability} and \citet{brennan2001generalizability} provide the canonical framework for multi-facet reliability analysis. \citet{mushquash2006gtheory} extended G-theory to binary data, and \citet{jiang2018bayesian} developed logistic-link generalized G-theory for binary outcomes. \citet{tuerlinckx2006glmm} established the connection between generalized linear mixed models and G-theory variance components. These methods ground our use of Henderson Method~I for binary correctness scores (\S\ref{sec:binary}).
15	
16	\paragraph{Positioning.}
17	Relative to \citet{bouthillier2021accounting}, who established ANOVA-based variance decomposition for ML benchmarks, we adopt G-theory's D-study framework, which enables prospective design optimization---predicting the number of items and conditions needed for a target reliability \emph{before} data collection. Our multi-benchmark design reveals format-associated structure that single-benchmark studies cannot detect. Relative to IRT approaches \citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks, zhou2026irt}, G-theory and IRT optimize orthogonal dimensions of evaluation design: IRT selects informative items, while G-theory determines the conditions needed for a target reliability. This is an illustrative comparison across different optimization objectives---IRT optimizes item discrimination, G-theory optimizes cross-facet generalizability. Relative to isolated-factor studies \citep{yuan2025layercast, sclar2024formatting, brittlebench2026, madaan2024quantifying}, our design crosses infrastructure and methodology facets within a single factorial framework, enabling interaction estimation that per-factor studies cannot provide.
```

</details>

On the methodology side, \\citet{sclar2024formatting} found up to 76\\% accuracy swings from prompt formatting changes on fixed hardware, and BrittleBench \\citep{brittlebench2026} reported 50\\% variance from prompt perturbation. \\citet{hua2025flaw} argued that apparent prompt sensitivity may partly be an evaluation artifact of log-likelihood scoring; our generation-based protocol controls for this confound, and our prompt variance component ($<$3\\% on MC benchmarks) is consistent with their finding that generation-based evaluation is more robust. \\citet{pezeshkpour2024ordering} showed that answer ordering in multiple-choice benchmarks introduces systematic bias, while \\citet{bui2025seeds} and \\citet{biderman2024lessons} documented sensitivity to random seeds and training configurations. Each of these studies isolates one factor while holding others fixed, which precludes estimating interactions between sources or comparing their relative magnitudes within a unified design.",
          " ",
          " \\paragraph{IRT and Efficient Benchmarking.}",
          " Item Response Theory (IRT) offers a complementary perspective on evaluation design by modeling item difficulty and discrimination parameters. \\citet{lalor2019irt} introduced IRT-based item analysis for NLP benchmarks, and \\citet{rodriguez2021leaderboards} showed that IRT reveals hidden properties of evaluation items that raw accuracy scores obscure. Building on this foundation, \\citet{polo2024tinybenchmarks} developed tinyBenchmarks, using IRT-based subsampling to reduce evaluation cost while preserving ranking accuracy, and \\citet{zhou2026irt} recommended a 116-item subset via PSN-IRT for efficient MMLU evaluation. \\citet{perlitz2023efficient} explored related strategies for benchmark compression. These approaches optimize item selection for latent trait estimation but do not model variance from prompt, seed, or ordering facets. IRT-selected items evaluated under a single methodological condition can therefore systematically underestimate the number of items required for reliable measurement across diverse evaluation settings."
        ]
      }
    ],

These methods ground our use of Henderson Method~I for binary correctness scores (\\S\\ref{sec:binary}).",
    "newString": "Our application of G-theory to binary correctness scores draws on established psychometric methodology. \\citet{shavelson1991generalizability} and \\citet{brennan2001generalizability} provide the canonical framework for multi-facet reliability analysis; \\citet{embretson2000irt} formalize differential item functioning (DIF), which we operationalize as model$\\times$item interaction. \\citet{mushquash2006gtheory} extended G-theory to binary data, and \\citet{jiang2018bayesian} developed logistic-link generalized G-theory for binary outcomes. \\citet{tuerlinckx2006glmm} established the connection between generalized linear mixed models and G-theory variance components. \\citet{xiao2025construct} and \\citet{jin2025psychometrics} provide complementary psychometric perspectives on LLM evaluation, addressing construct validity and measurement properties respectively. These methods ground our use of Henderson Method~I for binary correctness scores (\\S\\ref{sec:binary}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\\label{sec:related}\n\n\\paragraph{Variance Decomposition in ML/NLP Evaluation.}\nFactorial decomposition of evaluation variance has roots in the ML reproducibility literature. \\citet{bouthillier2021accounting} introduced an ANOVA-based template that decomposes benchmark variance across implementation details, hyperparameters, and data splits, establishing that design choices account for a substantial share of reproducibility failures. Several subsequent studies applied similar decomposition logic to specific facets of model evaluation \\citep{dodge2020finetuning, henderson2018deep, magnusson2023reproducibility}. \\citet{madaan2024quantifying} proposed per-factor variance metrics for LLM evaluation benchmarks but did not cross factors simultaneously. Recently, G-theory has been applied to LLM-based writing assessment \\citep{wei2025gtheory}, but this work addresses educational scoring consistency rather than benchmark evaluation variance where infrastructure and methodology facets interact. \\citet{kunievsky2025intent} proposed an alternative decomposition that separates intent uncertainty, articulation variance, and model uncertainty, but this framework does not cross infrastructure and methodology facets in a factorial design. Our work is, to our knowledge, the first to apply G-theory to LLM benchmark evaluation, crossing seven facets across five benchmarks to enable direct comparison of variance structures across evaluation formats.\n\n\\paragraph{Infrastructure and Prompt Variance.}\nA parallel line of work has examined individual variance sources in isolation. On the infrastructure side, \\citet{yuan2025layercast} documented up to 9\\% accuracy variation from numerical precision and GPU type but provided no factorial attribution. \\citet{messina2026background} formalized the concept of background temperature from floating-point non-associativity in GPU computation, and \\citet{thinkingmachines2025batch} proposed batch-invariant kernels to mitigate CUDA nondeterminism. On the methodology side, \\citet{sclar2024formatting} found up to 76\\% accuracy swings from prompt formatting changes on fixed hardware, and BrittleBench \\citep{brittlebench2026} reported 50\\% variance from prompt perturbation. \\citet{hua2025flaw} argued that apparent prompt sensitivity may partly be an evaluation artifact of log-likelihood scoring; our generation-based protocol controls for this confound, and our prompt variance component ($<$3\\% on MC benchmarks) is consistent with their finding that generation-based evaluation is more robust. \\citet{pezeshkpour2024ordering} showed that answer ordering in multiple-choice benchmarks introduces systematic bias, while \\citet{bui2025seeds} and \\citet{biderman2024lessons} documented sensitivity to random seeds and training configurations. Each of these studies isolates one factor while holding others fixed, which precludes estimating interactions between sources or comparing their relative magnitudes within a unified design.\n\n\\paragraph{IRT and Efficient Benchmarking.}\nItem Response Theory (IRT) offers a complementary perspective on evaluation design by modeling item difficulty and discrimination parameters. \\citet{lalor2019irt} introduced IRT-based item analysis for NLP benchmarks, and \\citet{rodriguez2021leaderboards} showed that IRT reveals hidden properties of evaluation items that raw accuracy scores obscure. Building on this foundation, \\citet{polo2024tinybenchmarks} developed tinyBenchmarks, using IRT-based subsampling to reduce evaluation cost while preserving ranking accuracy, and \\citet{zhou2026irt} recommended a 116-item subset via PSN-IRT for efficient MMLU evaluation. \\citet{perlitz2023efficient} explored related strategies for benchmark compression. These approaches optimize item selection for latent trait estimation but do not model variance from prompt, seed, or ordering facets. IRT-selected items evaluated under a single methodological condition can therefore systematically underestimate the number of items required for reliable measurement across diverse evaluation settings.\n\n\\paragraph{Binary Data and Psychometrics Foundations.}\nOur application of G-theory to binary correctness scores draws on established psychometric methodology. \\citet{shavelson1991generalizability} and \\citet{brennan2001generalizability} provide the canonical framework for multi-facet reliability analysis. \\citet{mushquash2006gtheory} extended G-theory to binary data, and \\citet{jiang2018bayesian} developed logistic-link generalized G-theory for binary outcomes. \\citet{tuerlinckx2006glmm} established the connection between generalized linear mixed models and G-theory variance components. These methods ground our use of Henderson Method~I for binary correctness scores (\\S\\ref{sec:binary}).\n\n\\paragraph{Positioning.}\nRelative to \\citet{bouthillier2021accounting}, who established ANOVA-based variance decomposition for ML benchmarks, we adopt G-theory's D-study framework, which enables prospective design optimization---predicting the number of items and conditions needed for a target reliability \\emph{before} data collection. Our multi-benchmark design reveals format-associated structure that single-benchmark studies cannot detect. Relative to IRT approaches \\citep{polo2024tinybenchmarks, zhou2026irt}, G-theory and IRT optimize orthogonal dimensions of evaluation design: IRT selects informative items, while G-theory determines the conditions needed for a target reliability. This is an illustrative comparison across different optimization objectives---IRT optimizes item discrimination, G-theory optimizes cross-facet generalizability. Relative to isolated-factor studies \\citep{yuan2025layercast, sclar2024formatting, brittlebench2026, madaan2024quantifying}, our design crosses infrastructure and methodology facets within a single factorial framework, enabling interaction estimation that per-factor studies cannot provide.",

Building on this foundation, \\citet{polo2024tinybenchmarks} developed tinyBenchmarks, using IRT-based subsampling to reduce evaluation cost while preserving ranking accuracy, and \\citet{zhou2026irt} recommended a 116-item subset via PSN-IRT for efficient MMLU evaluation. \\citet{perlitz2023efficient} explored related strategies for benchmark compression. _source line 2868 · 2026-05-22T03:54:19.271Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01MjeoRa6YayKB91A6NyaPQ1`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Method}
2	\label{sec:method}
3	
4	\subsection{Generalizability Theory}
5	\label{sec:gtheory}
6	
7	Generalizability theory (G-theory) treats each measurement as one observation from a universe of admissible conditions defined by the crossed facets of an evaluation design \cite{shavelson1991generalizability, brennan2001generalizability}. The \emph{object of measurement} is the entity whose consistency is of interest. In our setting, test items ($p$) serve as the object of measurement, and the goal is to determine how reliably item-level scores generalize across the methodological conditions under which they are collected.
8	
9	A \emph{G-study} decomposes total observed variance into components $\sigma^2_x$ for each facet $x$ and their interactions. Each component quantifies how much variability that source contributes to the overall measurement. Large interaction components (e.g., $\sigma^2_{pM}$ for item-by-model) indicate that facets do not affect items uniformly but instead create differential difficulty profiles. We estimate variance components via Henderson Method~I, an unbiased ANOVA-based estimator for balanced designs \cite[Ch.~3]{brennan2001generalizability}, with 1,000-resample bootstrap confidence intervals (item-level resampling with replacement, preserving the fully-crossed structure across all other facets) \cite{li2023bootstrap}.
10	
11	A \emph{D-study} projects the generalizability coefficient under hypothetical designs with different numbers of conditions per facet:
12	\begin{equation}
13	G_\text{item} = \frac{\sigma^2_p}{\sigma^2_p + \sigma^2_\text{rel}}
14	\label{eq:gcoeff}
15	\end{equation}
16	where $\sigma^2_\text{rel}$ aggregates all item-by-facet interaction variances, each divided by the number of conditions for the corresponding facets \cite[Ch.~4]{brennan2001generalizability}. This prospective capability distinguishes G-theory from classical ANOVA: rather than only attributing variance post hoc, a D-study enables optimization of evaluation designs \emph{before} data collection \cite{shavelson1989gtheory}. We define \emph{item-related variance} as the sum of the item main effect ($\sigma^2_p$) and the model$\times$item interaction ($\sigma^2_{pM}$). The latter captures differential item functioning (DIF): items whose difficulty ranking changes across models \cite[Ch.~4]{brennan2001generalizability}. Because DIF is operationally an item-level property---it reflects model-specific item difficulty profiles rather than a global model ability shift---aggregating $\sigma^2_p + \sigma^2_{pM}$ provides a unified measure of item-driven variance.
17	
18	Throughout this paper, we use the following notation: $p$ = item, $M$ = model, $\pi$ = numerical precision, $\delta$ = prompt template, $s$ = random seed, $\tau$ = sampling temperature, $o$ = answer ordering, and $\sigma^2_x$ = variance component for source $x$.
19	
20	\subsection{Experimental Design: MMLU}
```

{
  "replace_all": false,

  "old_string": "Throughout this paper, we use the following notation: $p$ = item, $M$ = model, $\\pi$ = numerical precision, $\\delta$ = prompt template, $s$ = random seed, $\\tau$ = sampling temperature, $o$ = answer ordering, and $\\sigma^2_x$ = variance component for source $x$.",
  "new_string": "\\paragraph{G-theory for ML practitioners.} In ML terms, a G-study is a multi-way ANOVA on evaluation outcomes where each ``facet'' is a design variable (prompt template, random seed, etc.) and variance components quantify each variable's contribution. A D-study is a what-if analysis: given these variance components, how many conditions per facet are needed to achieve a target reliability? The generalizability coefficient $G$ is analogous to a signal-to-noise ratio, where signal is the true item difficulty variance and noise is everything that changes across evaluation conditions. Unlike cross-validation, which assesses generalization to new data, G-theory assesses generalization across \\emph{measurement conditions}---the same items evaluated under different prompts, seeds, and models.\n\nThroughout this paper, we use the following notation: $p$ = item, $M$ = model, $\\pi$ = numerical precision, $\\delta$ = prompt template, $s$ = random seed, $\\tau$ = sampling temperature, $o$ = answer ordering, and $\\sigma^2_x$ = variance component for source $x$."
}

We choose Henderson Method~I over tetrachoric correlation approaches because the latter address a different problem---estimating latent continuous associations from observed binary data---and are not directly applicable to variance component estimation in multi-facet designs. REML estimates differ from Henderson Method~I by $<$0.5 percentage points on all components (Appendix~\\ref{app:robustness}), confirming estimator robustness."
}
```

  "replace_all": false,

  "old_string": "\\appendix\n\\section{Additional Robustness Analyses}\n\\label{app:robustness}\n\n\\textbf{Estimator robustness.} REML and Henderson Method~I differ by $<$0.5 percentage points on all components.\n\n\\textbf{CI method.} Bootstrap and jackknife confidence intervals agree closely (width ratio 0.96--1.04 across components).\n\n\\textbf{Facet specification.} Treating facets as fixed vs.\\ random changes $G_\\text{item}$ by only 0.37 percentage points.\n\n\\textbf{Leave-one-model-out (LOMO).} All eight seven-model subsets preserve the item + model$\\times$item dominance pattern (item\\_id 36--41\\%, $G_\\text{item}$ 0.877--0.889), confirming that the variance structure is robust to model composition.\n\n\\textbf{Null calibration.} A Bernoulli simulation with matched item difficulties produces a variance ratio of 1.0008 relative to observed data, confirming that the decomposition does not introduce systematic bias on binary outcomes.",
  "new_string": "\\appendix\n\\section{Additional Robustness Analyses}\n\\label{app:robustness}\n\nWe report five robustness analyses that verify the stability of the variance decomposition under alternative analytical choices.\n\n\\paragraph{Estimator robustness.} We compare Henderson Method~I with restricted maximum likelihood (REML-EM, 500 iterations, practical convergence $\\Delta < 0.003$ percentage points). All variance components differ by $<$0.5 percentage points between estimators. The largest discrepancy occurs on the residual component (0.47pp); all substantive components (item, model$\\times$item, model) differ by $<$0.3pp. Henderson Method~I is preferred for our balanced designs because it produces unbiased estimates without distributional assumptions \\cite[Ch.~12]{brennan2001generalizability}.\n\n\\paragraph{CI method comparison.} Bootstrap (1,000 item-level resamples with replacement) and delete-1 jackknife confidence intervals agree closely: width ratios range from 0.96 to 1.04 across all variance components. The agreement indicates that the dependence structure introduced by item-level bootstrap resampling (which creates $\\sim$127 unique items per 200-item resample) does not materially distort interval estimates.\n\n\\paragraph{Facet specification sensitivity.} Treating temperature and ordering as fixed (rather than random) facets changes $G_\\text{item}$ by only 0.37 percentage points (all-random: 0.820 vs.\\ fixed temperature+ordering: 0.824). The small difference reflects the negligible temperature$\\times$item (0.65\\%) and ordering$\\times$item (0.54\\%) variance shares. The random-facet assumption is therefore robust to facet classification.\n\n\\paragraph{Leave-one-model-out (LOMO).} All eight seven-model subsets preserve the item + model$\\times$item dominance pattern: item\\_id ranges from 36\\% to 41\\%, and $G_\\text{item}$ ranges from 0.877 to 0.889. No single model disproportionately drives the observed variance structure. The consistency of component rankings across all subsets supports the quasi-random model facet assumption (\\S\\ref{sec:model_facet}).\n\n\\paragraph{Null calibration.} We generate 100 Bernoulli-distributed datasets with item difficulties matched to the empirical distribution but no systematic facet effects. The observed-to-null variance ratio is 1.0008, confirming that Henderson Method~I does not introduce systematic bias when applied to binary outcomes. The null simulation also verifies that the item variance share (58.4\\%) is not an artifact of the Bernoulli $p(1-p)$ ceiling: the null produces item variance of 0.08\\%, three orders of magnitude below the observed value."
}
```

The random-facet assumption is therefore robust to facet classification.\n\n\\paragraph{Leave-one-model-out (LOMO).} All eight seven-model subsets preserve the item + model$\\times$item dominance pattern: item\\_id ranges from 36\\% to 41\\%, and $G_\\text{item}$ ranges from 0.877 to 0.889. No single model disproportionately drives the observed variance structure. The consistency of component rankings across all subsets supports the quasi-random model facet assumption (\\S\\ref{sec:model_facet}).\n\n\\paragraph{Null calibration.} We generate 100 Bernoulli-distributed datasets with item difficulties matched to the empirical distribution but no systematic facet effects. The observed-to-null variance ratio is 1.0008, confirming that Henderson Method~I does not introduce systematic bias when applied to binary outcomes. The null simulation also verifies that the item variance share (58.4\\%) is not an artifact of the Bernoulli $p(1{-}p)$ ceiling: the null produces item variance of 0.08\\%, three orders of magnitude below the observed value.\n"
}

    "content": "\\appendix\n\\section{Additional Robustness Analyses}\n\\label{app:robustness}\n\nWe report five robustness analyses that verify the stability of the variance decomposition under alternative analytical choices.\n\n\\paragraph{Estimator robustness.} We compare Henderson Method~I with restricted maximum likelihood (REML-EM, 500 iterations, practical convergence $\\Delta < 0.003$ percentage points). All variance components differ by $<$0.5 percentage points between estimators. The largest discrepancy occurs on the residual component (0.47pp); all substantive components (item, model$\\times$item, model) differ by $<$0.3pp. Henderson Method~I is preferred for our balanced designs because it produces unbiased estimates without distributional assumptions \\cite[Ch.~12]{brennan2001generalizability}.\n\n\\paragraph{CI method comparison.} Bootstrap (1,000 item-level resamples with replacement) and delete-1 jackknife confidence intervals agree closely: width ratios range from 0.96 to 1.04 across all variance components.
